#3157. Is Coca-Cola a Millionaire-Maker Stock?

Sometimes boring stocks have the brightest futures.

By Will Ebiefung – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Coca-Cola ›

Few businesses exemplify American business success more than Coca-Cola (KO +0.94%). Since its founding in the late 19th century, the multinational beverage maker has expanded globally to serve billions and become one of the world's most recognized brands. Investors have profited substantially along the way, and this shows little sign of stopping.

Over the last 12 months alone, shares have jumped by an impressive 35%, substantially outpacing the S&P 500's return of just 8% over the same time frame. This is a big deal for a company that is already large and mature. Let's dig deeper into the pros and cons of Coca-Cola to decide if the company still has millionaire-maker potential.

To understand Coca-Cola's appeal, it can pay to listen to the philosophy of one of its biggest backers, Warren Buffett. His Berkshire Hathaway portfolio owns a whopping $38.4 billion worth of Coca-Cola stock, making it his third-largest bet behind American Express and Apple.

Buffett likes to invest in companies with simple and easy-to-understand business models that enjoy a strong economic moat. A moat is a competitive advantage that protects a company's market share against competition and allows it to enjoy stable or elevated pricing power. Coca-Cola is arguably the quintessential example of this type of business.

You can walk into a convenience store or restaurant practically anywhere in the world and find Coca-Cola's flagship beverage Coke (or one of its many subbrands like Sprite, Fanta, and a variety of localized options).

The company's ubiquitous shelf space has created unparalleled habit creation among its consumers, which is something new rivals will struggle to disrupt, even if they manage to create better-tasting products. This advantage also makes Coca-Cola's sales remarkably recession-resistant. It has allowed the company to protect its margins amid soaring global inflation in raw materials like aluminum and sugar.

Coca-Cola's gross margins have hovered around 60%, despite the soaring prices for its products. This suggests the company is passing on the vast majority of inflation to consumers, while protecting its own profitability.

Coca-Cola isn't without some long-term challenges. The company is already a global brand name, and that means there is very little room for organic growth. Management will be increasingly tempted to rely on price increases to juice investor returns. While the company's gross margin of over 60% was easy to justify back when a 12-pack cost $4.00, it is much harder to stomach when prices have doubled, and more American families are struggling to make ends meet.

Soda prices have far exceeded the U.S. inflation rate, despite boasting a disproportionately middle- and lower-income consumer base. It's hard not to wonder if this situation is as sustainable as it may look on the surface.

Over the long term, there could be a generational shift away from brand-name soft drinks as people become more comfortable buying lower-cost store brands from major retailers like Walmart, which offers alternatives like Sam's Cola and Twist Up.

Coca-Cola's recent performance is impressive, and its strong moat can help set the company up for more market-beating growth over the long term. That said, with a market cap of $422 billion (in a mature industry with relatively little room for organic growth), Coca-Cola's days of explosive millionaire-making growth are likely behind it. Shares are also somewhat expensive at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple of 28.

Investors who buy the stock now should look at it as a safer alternative to more speculative opportunities like generative AI. The company's dividend (currently boasting a yield of 2.21%) will likely represent a sizable percentage of its long-term returns.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 768%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 199% for the S&P 500.

#3155. Pfizer's Launched and Acquired Drugs Grew 32% in Q3 — and 3 Other Reasons to Buy the Stock Before 2027

Pfizer remains deeply unloved on Wall Street, but it is slowly turning a corner.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Pfizer (PFE -0.51%) faces a large patent cliff, and investors worry it won't be able to offset the revenue it loses as older drugs face generic competition. That's a problem, but the drugmaker is aware of it and is making important strides in its efforts to address the issue. The big story, however, is that this issue is pretty normal for a drug company.

In fact, revenue from recently launched and acquired drugs rose 32% in the third quarter of 2026. That shows the pharmaceutical giant's efforts are bearing fruit. But there's more reason to be excited, including the company's strong balance sheet, its large dividend, and management's commitment to continue paying that dividend. Here's what you need to know.

One of the biggest worries about Pfizer is its upcoming patent expirations. Two big ones are Eliquis in 2028 and Vyndamax in 2031. But patent expirations are a normal part of the pharma sector. And research and development efforts in the highly technical and intensely competitive healthcare sector don't always align perfectly with a company's patent expiration schedule. It's just a fact of life.

The 32% revenue growth Pfizer achieved from launched and acquired products is evidence that the company is seeing some success in its efforts to address the issue. And it sets up three more reasons to consider buying the stock.

Pfizer ended the third quarter of 2026 with $1.8 billion in cash and $12.4 billion in short-term investments. That gives the company significant firepower to support its business through this patent-expiration cycle. In fact, the company's cash hoard is slightly higher than at the start of the year, so it isn't rapidly burning through its savings.

Despite the company's results with new drugs and its financial strength, investors are deeply negative on Pfizer's stock. That's highlighted by its huge 6.1% dividend yield as of this writing. To put that yield into context, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, and the average pharma stock yields 1.3%.

The negativity highlighted by the elevated yield is echoed by more traditional valuation metrics. Pfizer's price-to-sales and price-to-book ratios are below their five-year averages. And while the price-to-earnings ratio is elevated, it is largely due to one-time charges. Looking at the whole picture, Pfizer's stock looks attractively priced.

Given the lofty yield, Pfizer is likely to be most appealing to dividend-focused investors. But right now earnings aren't covering the dividend. That's not a great sign, but one-time charges are part of the issue. If you look at cash flow, the company can still support the dividend. That's highlighted by a cash dividend payout ratio of roughly 85%. While a lower number would be better, there's still some room for adversity. And don't forget about the company's large cash pile.

Both of these facts help support management's strong backing of the dividend. Indeed, one of Pfizer's key capital priorities is to "maintain and grow our dividend." Obviously, the company's priorities could change, but it wouldn't make a statement like that if the board of directors wasn't on board with protecting the dividend.

At the end of the day, Pfizer is facing very real headwinds today. But it is an industry-leading company with a long history of success. If history is any guide, it will muddle through this difficult period. The success it is having with new drugs, its financial strength, attractive valuation, and dedication to its large dividend are all strong reasons for investors to take a deep dive before 2026 is over. If you wait too long, the opportunity here may pass you by.

#3153. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, November 20: Noticeably Lower

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many home buyers.

Mortgage rates dropped today, but anything above 7% still puts pressure on home buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.24% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and three basis points lower than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

A significant drop is eye-catching, but you should consider mortgage interest rates' overall direction, not just what's going on today. While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday.

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Next week, the Nerds are waiting for the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, due out Wednesday. It's the Federal Reserve's preferred measure of inflation, so investors will be watching closely. The Fed doesn't set mortgage rates, but lenders anticipate its decisions, so mortgage rates often move before the Fed acts. Stubbornly high inflation is a big reason why we’re seeing upward pressure on mortgage rates right now.

If PCE shows inflation comes in hotter than expected, mortgage rates could climb as markets anticipate further Fed rate hikes. (An increase to the federal funds rate is already widely expected at the Fed’s Dec. 8-9 meeting.) A cooler reading could ease some pressure on mortgage rates for now.

I’m not getting my hopes up too high for meaningfully lower inflation, though. Last week's Producer Price Index showed wholesale inflation climbed 6.5% over the past year — its fastest pace in four years. Wholesale inflation measures the price businesses pay for things. When businesses’ costs go up, they could pass those along to consumers in the form of higher prices — keeping the flames of inflation alive.

Whether Wednesday's PCE brings relief or another helping of bad inflation news, we'll know before the turkey hits the table on Thursday.

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.74% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3151. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#3149. Stock Market Today, Nov. 20: Stocks Plunge as AI Trade Unwinds

Wall Street's worst stretch of the year deepened as AI-linked names sold off while defensive sectors drew buyers, today, Nov. 20, 2026.

By Emma Newbery – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Falling 3.70% to 6,910, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) entered a technical correction after a seven-day losing streak, while the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) dropped 5.28% to 22,618, erasing its annual gains. The Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -1.93%) fell 1.93% to 48,147.

Gold prices fell 3.02% to $3,517.24 as of U.S. market close, and the 10-Year Treasury yield slipped 0.10% to 4.91%. Sector-wise, energy and utility stocks gained, while technology and industrials tumbled.

Nvidia Corp (NVDA -14.01%) plummeted 14.01% following reports of a potential SEC accounting probe that accelerated a broader unwind of artificial intelligence trades. Broadcom Inc (AVGO -12.19%) tumbled 12.26% on unconfirmed reports that Alphabet's (GOOG -2.59%) Google would cut next year's chip shipments. Conversely, UnitedHealth Group Inc (UNH +2.83%)rose after TD Cowen upgraded the stock.

The sell-off in artificial intelligence (AI) stocks accelerated today, as simmering nerves boiled over into widespread loss of confidence. Nvidia's deepening woes only magnified the fallout. The Nasdaq Composite has fallen more than 10% this week, and the S&P 500 officially entered a technical correction after dropping 10% below its record high.

Institutional capital is rotating into defensive sectors, including retail and consumer staples. Walmart Inc (WMT +1.51%)gained after raising its full-year forecast in yesterday's earnings. Investors will be watching holiday grocery spending for signs of consumer resilience.

For investors, these dramatic shifts can be scary. It is important to remember that markets have always recovered, even from serious sell-offs, and that in the long term, what matters is staying invested. Holding a diversified portfolio of quality stocks for at least five years is a proven way to build wealth.

#3143. BigBear.ai vs. Innodata: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

One is built on federal and defense contracts, the other on training AI models for big tech, and their financial profiles diverge sharply.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 5:29PM EST

10 stocks we like better than BigBear.ai ›

Choosing between these specialized AI plays requires balancing high-stakes government work against commercial data engineering growth. Investors must decide if BigBear.ai (BBAI -8.67%) or Innodata (INOD -8.91%) better suits their portfolio needs.

BigBear.ai provides complex decision intelligence tools for federal and defense agencies, while Innodata helps major tech firms train generative AI systems. Both companies operate in the rapidly evolving world of artificial intelligence but serve different markets. This comparison examines their financial health, customer concentration, and valuation to help you decide which stock fits your strategy.

BigBear.ai offers decision intelligence solutions that combine data from different sources to help leaders make faster choices. It serves critical sectors like national security, cybersecurity, and global supply chains. According to its latest annual report, a specific contract accounted for nearly 51% of consolidated revenue. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $142.1 million. This represented a revenue growth decline of approximately 1.5% compared to the prior period. The company reported a net loss of roughly $180.2 million during this same time frame.

Following its early 2026 debt conversion, the debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.0x, meaning the company carries virtually no debt relative to its equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term debts with short-term assets, is close to 1.8x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was negative at nearly $46.3 million.

Innodata operates as a global data engineering firm that prepares and evaluates data for large-scale AI systems. The company serves several high-profile technology leaders, with one customer in its digital data solutions segment accounting for nearly 58% of total revenue in 2025. This focus on the best small cap tech stocks in the data sector highlights its role in the generative AI boom.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $350.2 million. This reflects a revenue growth increase of nearly 46.9% over the previous year. The company also achieved a net income of roughly $48.5 million during this period.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the current ratio is approximately 2.7x. The debt-to-equity ratio is 0.0x, indicating a strong balance sheet with no total debt. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was nearly $35.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented roughly 23.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

BigBear.ai faces significant risk due to heavy revenue concentration with government customers who can terminate contracts for convenience. Scaling its sales efforts remains a challenge while managing long and unpredictable sales cycles. It also faces intense competition from Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) and Amazon (AMZN -4.10%). The company also navigates complex AI deployment risks and recent financial volatility including litigation over accounting errors.

Innodata relies heavily on a single customer that contributed nearly 58% of its 2025 revenues, making it vulnerable to contract changes. It competes against well-resourced firms like Accenture (ACN +1.19%) and Cognizant Technology Solutions (CTSH +3.11%) in the data engineering space. The business also deals with geopolitical risks from its global workforce and ongoing legal challenges regarding securities class actions.

Innodata appears to offer a more attractive value proposition based on its lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with Innodata. AI models are only as good as the data they learn from, and Innodata helps big tech companies create and test that data. Its sales have grown year over year for 13 straight quarters, and it makes money.

Last quarter, sales jumped 53% and management raised its growth outlook, though the stock has fallen sharply this fall. There are risks: Two customers make up most of its sales, margins narrowed, and one program is winding down. But Innodata is adding new customers and work from a large cloud company, and expects to replace the lost program by early next year.

To give BigBear.ai its due, it does high-stakes work for national security and border security customers with contracts that are difficult to replace, and its backlog is growing. But it's still losing money, and government contract sales can be difficult to predict. That uncertainty is where Innodata stands apart: It is already profitable, with plenty of cash to keep investing as demand for AI data grows.

#3141. College Scholarship Breakdown: Types, Sponsors and Eligibility at a Glance

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships vary in what they pay for and who can apply. Understanding the differences can save you time during your application process.

Scholarships have become a standard part of applying for college. You can learn about scholarships in a variety of ways, but looking through an online database of available scholarships — the most common way — can be overwhelming.

Before you go searching for a scholarship, it's important to understand the types of scholarships available and how to identify the ones that give you the best chance of winning, which can save you time and potentially earn you more funding. Here’s what you need to know about scholarships to decide which ones are best for you.

Scholarships are often funded by three groups: private organizations, institutions like universities, and government agencies. None is better than the other when it comes to awarding funds.

Nonprofit organizations and clubs that are looking for students either majoring in or researching certain fields, or who have experience volunteering in a specific field.

Companies that want to support all students or only fund those majoring in a field that relates to the companies’ industries.

Financial institutions, such as banks or credit unions, that want to support their community or their members.

Individuals or families that are funding private scholarships, usually in memory of a loved one.

Universities and colleges that are offering funds to students who attend their campuses.

Research organizations that want to fund students who will research in their areas of interest and further their causes.

These scholarships generally are provided by local, state or federal government agencies for a variety of reasons. Despite being funded by a government agency, the scholarships do not require you to pay back the money — unlike federal student loans.

Some scholarships pay for anything related to college expenses, including tuition, room and board, books and supplies, and more. Other scholarships limit what you can pay for with the funds, such as only tuition or tuition along with books. It’s important to look at this restriction when applying for scholarships to ensure the funds will go toward what you need to cover.

Merit scholarships require you to show excellence or achievements in your schoolwork, through talents and abilities, and even leadership or service work. Because these types of awards can be highly competitive, merit scholarships might require more than just achievements, such as volunteer or leadership experience. This means merit-based scholarships can overlap with other types of scholarship categories. Here are some examples of merit-based scholarships:

Merit scholarships often require you to have earned a minimum grade point average (GPA). The minimum GPA depends on the scholarship but often ranges from 2.0 to 4.0, with more competitive scholarships usually requiring higher GPAs. Scholarships with lower GPA minimums are more likely to consider additional factors, such as financial need.

Some merit scholarships might require a minimum ACT or SAT score instead of a GPA — or in addition to a minimum GPA.

Merit scholarships might also require applicants to have participated in activities outside of their schoolwork. This is common for scholarships that require membership in a certain group or organization. For example, membership in agricultural clubs, such as 4-H or FFA, and scouting groups, such as the National Eagle Scout Association, appear in scholarship requirements.

Some scholarships emphasize volunteering or community service and only consider applicants who have that experience. Applications vary in how detailed their requirements can be. Some scholarships want only a general description of where and how you’ve volunteered, and others might require experience with a service project that shows more leadership experience or time volunteering with a certain type of organization.

Scholarships that are awarded to students who can’t afford college are called need-based scholarships.

Unmet financial need is the difference between what it will cost you to attend college and what you can afford to pay. Because calculating how much you can afford is a complicated process, many scholarships refer to your FAFSA to document your financial need.

Some scholarship requirements may be as straightforward as demonstrating that you have financial need, but some scholarships might have income limits. However, income restrictions are less common.

Athletic scholarships are for — you guessed it — athletes. They are usually offered by colleges and universities to recruit athletes onto their collegiate teams and can be competitive. Which sports have scholarships available and the scholarship amounts vary by college.

Even though the main criterion for an athletic scholarship is being good at your sport, you’ll likely still need to show that you can keep up with your schoolwork. Most athletic scholarships require students to earn a minimum GPA or test scores to remain eligible while they’re in college.

Some scholarships are designated for students majoring in a general field, such as science or education, but others can be for very specific areas of study. For example, a scholarship for students majoring in a natural resource field could include majors in biology, ecology, wildlife biology, conservation education, forestry, soils and hydrology. However, a stricter scholarship might state that students have to major in more specific topics, such as biochemistry or microbial ecology.

Scholarships are available for active and former members of the military and members of their families. In addition to federal funds available for veterans to attend college, private organizations and some universities have scholarships for veterans to offset the cost of a degree.

Which family members can apply for funds depends on the individual scholarship. For example, some scholarships extend to children or spouses of veterans, but some go as far in a family tree as great-grandchildren.

Veteran- and military-focused scholarships might require that applicants or their family members enlisted in a certain branch, served during a particular time or conflict, or meet a certain rank or status such as receiving disability. Scholarships might also require that the applicant be a wartime veteran or family member of a wartime veteran; not all veterans meet this status.

Scholarships are also awarded to people who identify with a certain group. Common examples of identity-based scholarships include those for people who have disabilities, belong to certain ethnic or religious groups, identify as LGBTQIA+ or are first-generation students. Some identity-based scholarships require applicants to meet several criteria, such as belonging to more than one identity group.

Outside of what would be considered standard DEI groups, there are scholarships for applicants who identify with less common groups, such as veganism, homelessness and model railroad builders.

The best scholarships for you depend on your situation. For example, if you are a skilled athlete with a good academic record, going for an athletic scholarship might give you the most funding. On the other hand, if you’re going to research a topic that isn’t common, looking for scholarships that support your research topic might be your best bet because competition could be lower.

Good tactics to figure out which scholarships are worth your time include:

Think of the skills, talents, experience and identities that are unique to you. For example, being a child of a veteran immediately qualifies you for scholarships with fewer applicants than scholarships open to everyone. Likewise, if you embrace a certain ethnicity, identify with a specific group or have experience with a type of work, you might qualify for scholarships that appreciate and recognize those attributes more than the average scholarship.

Apply if you think you're eligible. Don’t apply to just a few scholarships and hope you get them. Instead, apply for as many scholarships as you can and for which you’re eligible. This increases your chances of receiving funds. Even small scholarships can make a big impact when you add several of them together.

Understand how you can use the money. If you already have a scholarship that covers only books, applying for the same type of scholarship isn’t helpful if you don’t need the extra funds. Instead, look for scholarships that cover the costs you have remaining.

Not sure where to look? Our sister site Scholarships360.org provides students with vetted scholarship opportunities. That would be a good place to start.

#3140. When To End A Technology Pilot: 19 Warning Signs

Technology pilots give organizations an opportunity to test new solutions on a limited scale, evaluate their potential and identify challenges before committing to a broader rollout. But not every pilot warrants further investment, and knowing when to end one can be just as important as knowing when to expand it.

For tech leaders, the challenge is distinguishing between setbacks that can be addressed by adjusting a pilot’s scope or timeline and more fundamental issues that call its viability into question. Below, members of Forbes Technology Council share warning signs that indicate it’s time to end a technology pilot rather than extend it, redesign it or move it into production.

A pilot without success criteria is not a pilot. Defining boundaries and expectations, and, importantly, being transparent, are critical to a pilot’s success. Users should also share any uncertainty during the pilot instead of waiting for it to pivot or improve. - Arunava Sen, Tata Communications America Inc.

A clear sign is when the pilot team cannot tell you what the technology accessed, what decisions it made, or what data it touched. If you cannot answer those questions in a controlled environment (test group), you will not answer them in production. - Michael Nicosia, Salt Security

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

One sign a technology pilot shouldn’t progress doesn’t involve the new tech itself but the tools teams already work with. If teams are working in siloed workflows with tools that don’t collaborate with one another, introducing new technology can drive up costs, increase complexity and hurt productivity. Connected tools and workflows are integral to the success of any new tech deployment. - Usman Shuja, Bluebeam

End a pilot when its success depends on hand-curated data that won’t exist in production. If someone cleaned the dataset, resolved definitions and fixed edge cases for the pilot, and no one owns doing that at scale in production, extending or redesigning the product in production won’t help. In many cases, technology isn’t the problem; the foundation is. Ask not whether the pilot worked, but what it worked on. - Prakash Reddy, Okta

If the pilot only works while its champion is in the room or while the vendor quietly does manual work behind the scenes, you’ve tested enthusiasm, not technology. Hand it to a team that didn’t build it for 30 days. If results collapse, you’d be pushing a dependency into production, not a tool. End it and keep the learnings. - Damian Wasserman, BEON.tech

When the team stops asking what we learned and starts asking when we get to stop, that’s the sign. A pilot without defined success criteria is avoidance. If you can’t name the specific business result it was supposed to prove or disprove, extending it won’t help. End it, define the outcome you actually need, and redesign around that from the start. - Ali Alkhafaji, APPLY

Organizations should end a technology pilot when they have full visibility of the tool’s usage and cost metrics but still cannot connect either to a meaningful business outcome. Adoption alone is not enough without a clear path to measurable value, and extending the pilot risks turning experimentation into ongoing spend without a business case. - Conal Gallagher, Flexera

A clear sign is when the people who were supposed to benefit stop asking about it. Pilots drag on because the sponsors like the idea, not because users need it. If the pilot group has quietly gone back to their old way of working and nobody complains when the tool goes down, there’s nothing to extend. Name the lesson, thank the team and end it. - Hastimal Jangid, Coozmoo Digital Solutions, Inc.

A pilot should be reconsidered when it performs well in a demo but can’t prove that it solves a real business problem. AI makes it easy to add capabilities, but more features don’t always mean more value. Teams need clear measures of success and feedback loops to understand what’s working and what’s not before investing further. - Tony Grout, M-Files

A clear sign to end a technology pilot is when it repeatedly fails to demonstrate measurable business value despite validated assumptions and reasonable adjustments. If the solution cannot deliver meaningful ROI, operational efficiency or strategic impact, extending it only increases sunk costs. Strong leadership means knowing when to stop, not just when to scale. - Kameshwar Singh, Cohesity

End a pilot if no effort was spent defining the vision and how value will be realized at scale. That’s like building a bridge from both ends without deciding where it should meet. - Venky Ramesh, LatentView Analytics

A pilot should end when success depends on conditions that production will never have: handpicked data, expert users, manual fixes, privileged integrations or constant vendor support. If removing that scaffolding collapses the value case, extending the pilot only perfects the illusion. The real test is not, “Does it work?” but, “Does it still create measurable value when exposed to ordinary users, messy data, real constraints and an ordinary day?” - Nicola Sfondrini, PWC

End a pilot when making the technology operable, reliable and governable outweighs its business value. If protecting sensitive data, maintaining an audit trail and providing human oversight would cost more than the expected benefit, extending that particular pilot needs a stronger business case than the time and money already invested. - Brian Stimpfl, S-Docs

End a pilot when the evidence invalidates its core business premise. If users can complete the workflow but gain no meaningful time, cost or quality improvement over the current process, more engineering won’t create demand. Define that minimum benefit before launch, test it with real users, and stop when it remains out of reach. - Irina Shymko, Langate Software

End the pilot when the team cannot define a credible path from the experiment to a measurable business outcome. If success still depends on vague future improvements, heroic manual support or assumptions the pilot was supposed to validate, extending it usually just delays the decision. - Dhyey Mavani, Abridge

A pilot should end when the team cannot explain what new evidence another extension will produce. Every extra week should answer a specific open question about value, risk, adoption, cost or scale. Once the remaining time is only keeping the pilot alive, the experiment has already delivered its answer. - Sibasis Padhi, Walmart Inc.

I would end a pilot when it successfully completes individual tasks but repeatedly fails to deliver the full business outcome. Often, the underlying issue is the absence of a clear decision model for handling exceptions, approvals or unresolved work across teams. If nobody can authorize what happens next, scaling the technology only scales the problem. - Vamshi Noothi, Cisco

A pilot should be ended if nobody uses it unless someone reminds them. A pilot that only stays alive while its sponsor keeps pushing won’t survive production, and extending it just postpones that answer. Software nobody uses isn’t free; it still costs money to run, maintain and support. End it, write down what you learned about the problem, and put the team on something people actually want. - Jeremy Suriel, Kustomer

A clear sign to end a technology pilot is when no business owner is willing to take responsibility for implementation. Even if the technology performs well, success requires business ownership, accountability and commitment to change. Without a sponsor willing to champion the solution, allocate resources and own the outcomes, extending the pilot is unlikely to deliver value. Technology cannot create lasting benefits without clear business ownership. - Eranda Maldeniya, Enterprise Analytics

#3139. Stock Market Today, Nov. 20: Amazon Shares Slide on AI and Mega-Cap Tech Sell-Off

On Nov. 20, 2026, Amazon shares fell as investors weighed heavy AI spending and margins amid a broad tech selloff.

#3137. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#3135. Why Globant Stock Flew Higher on a Lousy Friday for the Market

It posted an encouraging third-quarter earnings report.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:48PM EST

Globant (GLOB +4.13%) was one of the rare stocks to rise amid the sharp decline of the wider market on Friday. It acquired that port-in-a-storm quality on its latest set of quarterly results, which were good enough to draw investors to its equity. The digital transformation specialist's shares ended the day more than 4% higher.

Globant's third-quarter revenue was $611.1 million, down 1% year over year. Non-IFRS (adjusted) net income also declined, falling by almost 9% to nearly $63.8 million, or $1.48 per share.

That earnings per share (EPS) figure met analyst expectations while revenue notched a slight beat. The pundit top-line consensus for that line item was $610.9 million.

Like the more progressive legacy software companies, Globant -- which specializes in developing custom software for enterprises automating aspects of their business -- has embraced artificial intelligence (AI) in its solutions. It said in its earnings release that its recently introduced AI Pods subscription service had doubled its annualized run rate.

Globant quoted co-founder and CEO Martin Migoya as saying that "We are seeing growing enterprise adoption as clients shift from hourly billing toward outcome- and consumption-based delivery."

It's one thing to embrace AI as either a tool for internal software development or to tack on some of its functionalities to an existing software suite. It's quite another to lead with it as a product offering, like Globant is doing with AI Pods.

The service is clearly resonating with the market, and I think it might soon become a driver of growth for this company given its relatively high margins. This is starting to look something like a sleeper stock among the many software titles on the market, and as such, it's well worth watching.

#3132. Here's the Average Social Security Benefit at Age 67

Full retirement age is the baseline for your check, but where most 67-year-olds actually land may surprise you.

By Kailey Hagen, CFP® – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:00PM EST

The average Social Security benefit for 67-year-olds is $2,016.48 per month, according to Social Security Administration (SSA) data from December 2025. Full retirement age (FRA) ranges from 66 to 67, depending on your birth year, and it's 67 for everyone born in 1960 or later. It's the point where you're eligible for your unreduced retirement amount.

Reaching this age doesn't always mean you receive exactly 100% of your baseline amount. Many people who are 67 today have a full retirement age of 66 years and 10 months. These workers may have already earned small delayed retirement credits if they waited until 67 to claim their checks.

Earnings histories also impact these averages. Men who are 67 receive an average of $2,234.41 per month, while the average among women is $1,801.82. This results in a monthly gender gap of $432.59 as of December 2025.

Think of your full retirement age as the baseline for your benefits. The SSA bases your check on your average monthly income over your 35 highest-earning years, adjusting those wages for inflation and applying the primary insurance amount formula. This calculation creates the standard amount you're entitled to at full retirement age.

You don't have to claim at your baseline. You can earn delayed retirement credits, which are increases for delaying past FRA, worth 8% per year up to age 70. For a worker whose FRA is 67, waiting those three extra years provides a 24% total increase in monthly income.

These decisions have a visible impact on the data. The average monthly benefit at age 70 is $2,274.68, which is $258.20 higher than the age-67 average.

But even for these claimants, benefits alone often aren't enough to live on. Social Security was only designed to replace about 40% of pre-retirement wages, so most retirees rely on savings or other income to cover their full costs.

A 3.6% cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) will take effect in 2027 to help benefits keep pace with inflation. This will add about $73 a month on top of the average listed above. These adjustments are designed to protect your purchasing power over time.

Knowing the population average gives you a target for your own planning, but your final check depends on your career earnings and when you choose to claim.

Waiting until age 67 to claim Social Security helps you avoid the early claiming benefit reduction, which can drastically reduce your checks. This is one major reason FRA is such a popular claiming age, but it's not the only one.

When you claim Social Security under your FRA, you're also at risk of losing some of your benefits to the earnings test if you're still working. In 2027, for example, you lose $1 from your checks for every $2 you earn over $25,960 if you're under your FRA all year. If you'll reach your FRA next year, you only lose $1 for every $3 you earn over $67,920.

These lost benefits come back to you as a permanent benefit boost at your FRA. But for some, the risk of short-term losses makes them delay their claim until their FRA, when the earnings test no longer applies. This is something to weigh if you plan to continue working once you're eligible for Social Security checks.

For more on how Social Security affects your retirement, see this guide.

#3124. PayPal vs. SoFi Technologies: Which Financial Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

PayPal's global reach and SoFi's shift to profitability pull in opposite directions, and the valuation gap between them may tip the debate for 2027 investors.

By Sara Appino – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 6:05PM EST

Deciding between PayPal (PYPL -5.46%) and SoFi Technologies (SOFI -7.76%) requires balancing established market dominance against aggressive digital growth. Both companies are fighting for a larger share of your digital wallet today.

PayPal remains a global giant in payment processing, while SoFi is a fast-growing digital bank targeting a younger, high-earning demographic. While they operate in different niches, both compete for the same pool of consumer financial activity, making them frequent candidates for a fintech-focused portfolio as investors look toward the future of money.

PayPal operates a massive digital payments platform serving nearly 439 million active accounts across 200 markets. It provides branded checkout services, person-to-person payments via Venmo, and even Bitcoin (CRYPTO:BTC) trading within its app. This scale makes it one of the most recognizable names in fintech stocks today as it adapts to a changing financial landscape.

Reliable recent quarterly figures for at least one of these companies aren't available, so this comparison uses each company's latest full fiscal year. For FY 2025, PayPal reported revenue of nearly $33.2 billion, representing growth of approximately 4.3% over the prior year. The company generated net income of close to $5.2 billion, showing a steady ability to convert its high volume of transactions into profit for its shareholders.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio reached roughly 0.5x, comparing total debt to shareholder equity. The current ratio, which compares short-term assets to short-term liabilities, was approximately 1.3x. Free cash flow, or the cash remaining after paying for operations and equipment, was nearly $5.6 billion in FY 2025.

SoFi Technologies is a mobile-first financial services provider that offers a wide range of products including student loan refinancing, home loans, and investment tools. It serves approximately 15.8 million members and has expanded into business services through a partnership with Mastercard (MA -0.14%). The company also supports Bitcoin trading and provides modern banking infrastructure to other firms through its proprietary technology segments.

For its latest annual report covering FY 2025, SoFi Technologies generated revenue of nearly $3.6 billion, which was an increase of approximately 35.1% compared to the previous year. The company reported net income of close to $481.3 million, marking a significant transition into consistent profitability. This growth was driven by a diversified mix of lending products and financial services that attracted new members to its platform.

Based on its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was roughly 0.2x, helping investors see the low level of debt relative to shareholder equity. The current ratio was approximately 0.2x, which measures a company's ability to cover immediate liabilities with assets that can be quickly converted to cash. Free cash flow for the year was a net loss of nearly $4.0 billion, which often occurs in banking as capital is deployed into new loans rather than held as cash.

PayPal faces intense competition from both traditional banks and newer technology platforms that offer digital checkout solutions. The company is also subject to extensive global regulatory oversight regarding payment processing and anti-money laundering rules. Any failure to manage cybersecurity risks or protect user data could lead to significant penalties and damage its reputation.

SoFi Technologies must manage the complex regulatory requirements that come with being a bank holding company. It relies on a concentrated number of whole loan purchasers, meaning its results could suffer if these buyers reduce their activity. Additionally, the company faces the challenge of managing rapid growth across its various lending and technology segments without increasing credit risks.

When comparing these two companies using Forward P/E, PayPal looks significantly cheaper than its peer based on future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

I'd go with SoFi Technologies. Its sales and earnings are both growing far faster than PayPal's, and it has built a full digital bank with more than 17 million members, starting from student loans. Its bank charter lets it fund loans with its members' deposits, a cost advantage most fintech rivals lack.

SoFi also gave investors its 2027 outlook a full quarter early, and it came in above analysts' expectations, with revenue expected to grow about 27%. Management built it assuming interest rates keep rising, even after a rate hike this year hurt the stock. The shares are still down for the year, so long-term investors get a lower price for a profitable, growing business.

To give PayPal its due, it is a household name that produces plenty of cash, and Venmo is growing fast. But its original business, letting shoppers pay online with their PayPal account, is barely growing, and overall sales are rising slowly. By contrast, SoFi's lending and banking businesses are surging, and even its technology business, which lost a large client, is expected to return to growth in 2027. For long-term investors, that mix of growth, profits, and a lower price makes SoFi the better buy for 2027.

#3117. Prediction: Rocket Lab Will Triple Its Revenue by 2028. Here’s What That Would Mean for the Stock.

What sounds outlandish on the surface actually makes a lot of sense once you dive deeper into the details of this space-launch company’s efforts.

By James Brumley – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Rocket Lab ›

Plenty of companies are growing incredibly well right now, including several from the artificial intelligence business. A company that's set to triple its top line over the course of the next couple of years, though? That seems outrageous.

Except for orbital launch and satellite tech specialist Rocket Lab (RKLB -10.92%), it isn't. This future business is already lined up. These paying customers are just waiting for one pivotal milestone that's likely to materialize sometime next year. Then the floodgates open.

Rocket Lab offers satellite launch solutions. Its relatively small Electron rocket design has now been launched 100 times, putting 273 satellites into low earth orbit.

The organization also provides a range of satellite components and construction services, which accounts for more than half of last quarter's revenue of $268 million. Rocket Lab expects to do around $980 billion in business in fiscal 2026, well up from last year's $602 billion.

This is just a taste of what's to come, though. As the company announced with its third-quarter report posted earlier this month, Rocket Lab's backlog grew 52% from Q2's $2.36 billion to an incredible $3.6 billion. The organization just needs more capacity, but that's coming. Rocket Lab can now manufacture an Electron rocket every eight days.

Then there's the proverbial 800-pound gorilla in the room. That's the company's larger Neutron rocket, which has seen its inaugural flight pushed back several times, with the first flight now expected sometime in 2027.

Despite the delays so far, once Neutron is flying, everything takes a turn for the better. As CFO Adam Spice commented during the third-quarter's earnings conference call, adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) "will turn positive within six months of that first successful test flight," with cash flow likely to turn positive shortly after that.

Neutron isn't the big profit center in and of itself, to be clear. It's just a means to an end. Being able to put larger, heavier satellites into orbit also opens up its satellite tech business. Of its new backlog of $3.6 billion, $1.7 billion of it reflects space systems rather than launch services, up 194% year over year.

To this end, while analysts aren't yet looking for Rocket Lab's revenue to triple between now and 2028, with recent failures of similar rockets like ULA's Vulcan and one earlier in the year from Blue Origin, Rocket Lab is quickly becoming a preferred launch option. It may well exceed 2028's expectations.

As for the impact on the stock, it's not a complicated prediction to make. If the company's top line comes anywhere close to tripling by 2028, the bullish argument firms up enormously.

While it may not triple in value in step with revenue, $3 billion in sales could turn this high-growth $34 billion outfit into a $60 billion outfit with relative ease. And given that the company isn't likely to raise money by issuing more stock anytime soon, that translates into a near-doubling of the stock's price.

You're getting some help in the meantime, by the way. The analyst community overwhelmingly considers RKLB stock a strong buy right now, with a 12-month consensus price target of $93.30 already more than 80% above the ticker's current price.

#3111. Prediction: Nvidia Stock Will Do This in the 6 Months After a New CEO Takes Over, According to History

Nvidia is searching for a new CEO following Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 20, 2026 at 6:42PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) is navigating one of the most consequential leadership transitions in recent Silicon Valley history. Following founder Jensen Huang's unexpected resignation amid a controversy involving alleged connections to Chinese intelligence, investors are growing increasingly concerned about the company's future. This uncertainty is weighing heavily on Nvidia stock, even as its underlying business continues to deliver extraordinary financial results.

Colette Kress, Nvidia's former chief financial officer, is now serving as interim CEO while the board searches for a permanent successor. According to reports, the company is considering external candidates, including Arm Holdings CEO Rene Haas.

History offers some clues about how technology stocks behave following major CEO transitions. However, Nvidia's circumstances are quite different from those of its peers, making the company's financial outlook particularly important right now.

Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%) represents a successful example of a technology leadership transition. Satya Nadella became CEO on Feb. 4, 2014, replacing Steve Ballmer after Microsoft conducted a formal succession search. Nadella had previously led Microsoft's cloud and enterprise businesses, giving him extensive knowledge of the company's operations.

Microsoft stock advanced roughly 19% during Nadella's first six months as CEO. Of course, over the long term, he helped transform the Windows developer into a cloud computing powerhouse.

Alphabet (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%) offers another example. Sundar Pichai became CEO of Alphabet on Dec. 3, 2019, succeeding co-founder Larry Page. However, Pichai had already served as Google's CEO since 2015, making the transition more of a consolidation of leadership responsibilities than an unexpected management overhaul.

Alphabet stock was higher roughly six months later, despite experiencing a meaningful drawdown during the short-lived COVID-19 market crash. Moreover, Page and his co-founder Sergey Brin remained involved as shareholders and directors, providing continuity that Nvidia currently lacks.

Amazon (AMZN -4.10%) experienced a different outcome when Andy Jassy succeeded Jeff Bezos on July 5, 2021. Jassy was another internal executive who had previously built and led Amazon Web Services (AWS). Although Bezos relinquished the CEO position, he remains executive chairman, preserving his influence over the company's long-term strategy.

Amazon shares declined approximately 6% during Jassy's first six months as CEO. At the time, investors were assessing the impacts of slowing pandemic-era growth and rising operating expenses on Amazon's e-commerce marketplace.

The three examples above show that CEO succession can bring different short-term outcomes. However, they also share an important characteristic: Each of these companies promoted an experienced internal executive through an orderly transition, unlike Nvidia's abrupt leadership disruption.

Nvidia's situation is unusual because Huang wasn't just another corporate executive. He co-founded Nvidia in 1993 and spent more than three decades directing the company's evolution from a graphics chip manufacturer into the world's leading supplier of artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure. His influence extended across engineering, product development, corporate culture, and customer relationships.

As a result of his departure, investors are increasingly questioning whether Nvidia can maintain its technological edge without the executive who helped establish it. The uncertainty is compounded by growing competition from custom AI accelerators developed by Alphabet, Amazon, and other hyperscalers. Nvidia's next CEO needs to prove they can preserve the company's innovation-driven culture while navigating complicated supply chains and regulatory relationships.

Despite the unusual circumstances, Nvidia's financial outlook provides a compelling reason to anticipate a meaningful recovery. During its fiscal third-quarter earnings call, management increased its fiscal 2028 revenue growth forecast to 75%, up from its previous outlook of 70%. This suggests demand for Nvidia's accelerated computing platforms remains strong despite Huang's departure.

Furthermore, Nvidia expects to generate $130 billion in revenue during the fourth quarter -- representing 91% growth year over year. The company is also forecasting gross margins of between 72% and 73% in fiscal 2028, suggesting that accelerating sales growth will translate into additional profits.

Nvidia's share repurchase program provides another subtle catalyst for the stock. In September, the board increased the company's existing authorization by $150 billion, leaving approximately $227.3 billion available at the end of the third quarter. Management intends to execute the remaining authorization by the end of fiscal 2028.

By reducing the number of outstanding shares, stock buybacks can increase earnings per share (EPS) even if net income is unchanged. Combined with Nvidia's revenue growth and profit margins, upcoming share repurchases should fuel substantial EPS expansion over the coming year.

My prediction is that Nvidia stock will trade higher six months after its new CEO assumes the role. The appointment itself should remove the primary source of uncertainty around the company, allowing investors to concentrate once again on revenue growth, earnings power, and competitive advantages.

#3107. Here's How Much Nvidia Stock Is Worth Without Jensen Huang, According to Dan Ives

Dan Ives revised his price target on Nvidia stock following Jensen Huang's resignation as CEO.

By Adam Spatacco – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:10PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) has experienced a stunning reversal following founder Jensen Huang's unexpected resignation amid controversy surrounding alleged connections to Chinese intelligence. Investors are increasingly questioning whether the semiconductor behemoth can maintain its technological edge without the executive who spent more than three decades building the company. Those concerns have contributed to a dramatic sell-off, even as Nvidia continues reporting ridiculously good financial results.

The magnitude of the decline is anything but trivial. Nvidia stock reached a record closing price of around $239 in early October but has fallen to roughly $136 as of Nov. 20. That represents a decline of roughly 43%, wiping out more than $2.5 trillion in market capitalization and pushing Nvidia from the world's most valuable public company to fourth place.

Nevertheless, Wall Street analyst Dan Ives thinks investors are overlooking Nvidia's underlying earnings power. Although he acknowledges that Huang's departure changes the company's investment thesis, his revised price target suggests meaningful upside remains.

Ives has been particularly vocal about the consequences of Huang's resignation. During a recent interview, he estimated that Huang contributed $1 trillion to Nvidia's valuation. In a separate appearance, Ives described Huang as the company's "heart and lungs," emphasizing his influence over Nvidia's technological roadmap and competitive position.

Ives says Nvidia stock is worth $225 without Huang and $300 with him as CEO. These figures provide an interesting way to quantify the so-called Jensen premium. The difference between Ives' two valuations is $75 per share, representing a 25% reduction from his original $300 target.

The implied difference in market capitalization is actually considerably larger than the $1 trillion Ives alluded to. With approximately 24.2 billion shares outstanding, a $75 difference translates into roughly $1.8 trillion in equity value. At Ives' respective price targets, Nvidia would command a market capitalization of approximately $7.3 trillion with Huang and $5.4 trillion without him.

The difference in valuation illustrates the enormous importance Wall Street places on Nvidia's founder and why investors have reacted so negatively to his departure.

Huang's value extends beyond his responsibilities as CEO. Since co-founding Nvidia in 1993, he has overseen the company's transformation from a graphics processing company into the dominant supplier of accelerated computing infrastructure. His investments in GPUs, CUDA software, and AI computing helped position Nvidia at the center of the technology industry's most important transformation since the birth of the internet.

Perhaps Huang's greatest contribution has been leading Nvidia's culture of innovation. Huang consistently anticipated computing trends years before they became commercially relevant. This enabled Nvidia to develop new chip architectures and software ecosystems that competitors have struggled to replicate. This foresight helps establish durable competitive advantages and contributes to the company's pricing power.

Huang also cultivated relationships with other technology executives, hyperscalers, governments, and AI labs. These supply chain connections have become important as Nvidia participates in data center build-outs that require complex financing arrangements and long-term strategic commitments.

With all of that said, Nvidia's competitive advantages are not going to disappear just because Huang stepped away. The company's hardware and software ecosystem, engineering workforce, customer relationships, and product roadmap remain intact.

Moreover, Ives acknowledges that Nvidia has a lead of two to three years over its competitors in AI chip technology. Although Huang's departure could narrow that advantage over time, the company's existing leadership provides a robust foundation for future growth.

Jensen Huang. Image source: Getty Images.

Despite reducing his Nvidia price target from $300 to $225, Ives maintains a bullish outlook on the company. At approximately $136 per share, his revised target implies roughly 65% upside, while his original $300 estimate implies 121% potential appreciation.

Importantly, Ives' optimism centers on the insatiable demand for AI computing infrastructure and Nvidia's technological advantages. Following the company's earnings report on Nov. 17, Ives wrote that the results answered a critical question: "Demand did not leave with him."

The financial evidence supports that assessment. Nvidia generated $115.8 billion in fiscal third-quarter revenue, representing 103% growth year over year, while data center revenue surged 110% to $107.6 billion. The company also reported a gross margin of 74.8%, demonstrating that its pricing power remains strong.

Furthermore, Nvidia forecast $130 billion in revenue for the fourth quarter and raised its fiscal 2028 revenue growth outlook to 75%. This forecast suggests the company is poised for further expansion despite the leadership transition.

The central takeaway from Ives' analysis is that Nvidia doesn't necessarily need Huang to remain an attractive investment. Although his departure has reduced -- for now -- the premium investors are willing to pay, his price target still suggests considerable appreciation over the coming year. For investors who can stomach short-term volatility, Nvidia's recent sell-off could represent an opportunity to buy the dip in the most dominant AI infrastructure business at a significantly discounted valuation.
