#3104. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3101. Bristol Myers Squibb's Growth Portfolio Just Hit $7.7 Billion. Is This Cheap Pharma Stock a No-Brainer Buy?

The good news is that Bristol Myers Squibb's new drugs are growing; the bad news is that one old drug is nearing its patent expiration.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Bristol Myers Squibb ›

Bristol Myers Squibb (BMY +0.79%) is a large, well-respected pharmaceutical company. It traces its history back to the mid-1800s. Right now, the company's portfolio of newer drugs, which it calls its "growth portfolio", just generated nearly $7.7 billion in revenue in a single quarter. That's a 12% increase over the same quarter in 2025. But is the stock worth buying? The risk/reward balance looks tilted toward reward for all but the most conservative investors. Here's why.

The success Bristol Myers Squibb is having with its growth portfolio clearly indicates that this pharmaceutical giant hasn't lost its mojo. But investors are still downbeat on the stock (more on this below). So the dividend yield is a lofty 4.3%. The S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, the average healthcare stock yields 1.3%, and the average drug stock yields 1.3%. That above-market and industry yield will likely be very enticing to long-term dividend investors. Meanwhile, the roughly 55% dividend payout ratio leaves ample room for adversity and should mollify all but the most conservative income lovers.

Notably, the dividend has been increased regularly, though not annually, for over three decades. There have been no dividend cuts along the way despite the company working through some difficult periods during that span. That shows the board of directors is strongly committed to returning value to shareholders through a reliable, slowly growing dividend. Adding to the allure is the fact that the stock's 13x price-to-earnings ratio is notably below the pharma industry average of roughly 28x. The high yield and relatively low P/E ratio will likely be of interest to value investors.

Growth investors will appreciate the above-highlighted success of the company's growth portfolio. That portfolio is a collection of over a dozen new drugs that, as of the third quarter, account for nearly 60% of the company's drug revenues. That's good news and bad news, however, and it highlights the risk that investors face if they decide to buy Bristol Myers Squibb.

The healthcare giant's legacy portfolio still accounts for around 40% of revenues, or roughly $5.4 billion. And one drug, Eliquis, is responsible for nearly $4.7 billion of that total. Eliquis is set to see generic competition as soon as 2028. When that happens, the revenue this drug produces will likely start to fall, and perhaps sharply. That is why investors remain downbeat on Bristol Myers Squibb's stock despite the success of its growth portfolio.

It is reasonable that extremely conservative investors might want to stay on the sidelines until after Eliquis' patent expires. However, it is important to remember that patent expirations are just a normal part of the pharmaceutical business. And given the success of the growth portfolio, Bristol Myers Squibb continues to execute well in new drug development. There's just a mismatch between when patents expire, which happens on a set schedule, and its development efforts, which do not happen on a set schedule.

In order to buy Bristol Myers Squibb, you need to believe it will get through this period. History suggests it will, and that's backed up by the success of the growth portfolio.

If you can't handle the uncertainty around the patent expiration of Eliquis, you shouldn't buy Bristol Myers Squibb. However, for most investors, including dividend, value, and perhaps even growth-focused types, the stock is probably worth a deep dive. The market doesn't appear to be giving the company enough credit for its success on the development front, and that is probably tilting the risk-reward scale toward reward.

#3098. The S&P 500 Has More Than Tripled in 10 Years, Even After Its Latest Pullback. Here’s What $1,000 in VOO Could Have Become.

A host of concerns are creating the perfect storm right now, harming market sentiment.

By Neil Patel – Nov 20, 2026 at 7:23PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

From the start of 2026 to its peak on Oct. 6, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%), the measuring stick for how the U.S. equity market is performing, posted a price return of 14%. Since that all-time high, though, the benchmark has fallen about 10% (as of Nov. 19).

Investors might be worried about a few things. They could be more skeptical about all the artificial intelligence (AI) data center spending that's taking place. Elevated inflation and rising interest rates are stealing the show from a macro perspective. And we're past a midterm election cycle that saw the Democrats take control of Congress, introducing regulatory uncertainty.

Step back. Even after its recent pullback, the S&P 500 index has risen 229% in 10 years. Here's what a $1,000 investment made in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -3.42%) a decade ago could have become today.

The S&P 500 index climbed at a 12.6% annualized rate over the past decade, before dividends. Including dividends, a $1,000 investment in the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF 10 years ago would be worth about $3,953 today. That's a 14.7% annualized total return. Investors almost quadrupled their money.

The recent drop is a blip on the big-picture radar. Even after sliding since early October, the index is still up slightly in 2026.

What powered the gains? It's mostly earnings. In December 2016, the S&P 500 traded at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 17. This compares the index's price to the profits analysts expect over the next 12 months. Today, the multiple is around 18, slightly below its 10-year average.

Based on this metric, the valuation barely moved. Forward earnings per share, meanwhile, jumped from $133 (at the end of 2016) to $416 in early November of this year, a 212% increase. Investors didn't make money by paying higher prices. They built wealth because American businesses became far more profitable.

One sector stands above the rest. Information technology, measured by the Vanguard Information Technology ETF (VGT -7.00%) delivered an annualized total return of roughly 22.7% in the last decade. AI, cloud computing, and surrounding internet-based secular trends made trillion-dollar market caps a common theme. The tech sector now represents 37% of the S&P 500 index. VOO investors own those winners automatically, for an expense ratio of just 0.03%.

Those gains weren't easy to hold on to. The S&P 500 index fell nearly 20% in late 2018, crashed 34% during the COVID-19 panic, slid 25% in the 2022 bear market, and dropped 19% during the 2025 tariff scare. This year alone has brought two declines of 8% or more. Volatility is just a part of the game.

Each time the market fell, selling felt like the right move. But each time, the index eventually bounced back and went on to new highs.

Investors who trade in and out usually pay for it. Morningstar's (MORN -0.30%) latest Mind the Gap study found that the average dollar in U.S. mutual funds and ETFs earned 8.7% per year over the decade ended 2025. The funds themselves returned 9.9%. The difference is due to poorly timed buying and selling.

Missing a few days hurts, too. According to J.P. Morgan (JPM -3.03%) Asset Management, sitting out the 10 best days over the 20-year period from 2006 through 2025 would have cut an investor's annualized return from 11% to 6.6%, an enormous difference.

Dollar-cost averaging is the solution. This means investing a set amount on a regular schedule, regardless of price. There's no guessing involved. Adding steadily in this manner to the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF is no doubt one of the simplest ways to build wealth.

When the market is down, like it is now, hang in there.The best results of the past decade came from holding through every pullback.

#3095. Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, ‘miserable' weeks

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

#3094. Report: Goldman Sachs Predicts the S&P 500 Will Gain 26% in 12 Months

Despite a rocky few weeks on Wall Street, the next 12 months are looking much more bullish, according to Goldman Sachs.

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The stock market has had a rough few weeks. As of Nov. 20, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) had declined by about 10.4% in the prior month, largely driven by investor worries over the sustainability of the artificial intelligence (AI) trade. The tech-heavy Nasdaq-100 index has declined almost enough to qualify as a bear market, with a drop of nearly 20% since its Oct. 6 record high.

But according to Bloomberg reporting from Nov. 13, Goldman Sachs (GS -6.71%) strategists are bullish on the S&P 500 for next year. Recent research from Goldman suggests that the benchmark S&P index will gain 26% in the next 12 months by November 2027, despite possibly lower earnings growth compared to 2026.Let's look at what this might mean for investors -- and why it's a good sign to keep buying stocks with low-cost index funds like S&P 500 ETFs.

Goldman strategists predict that the S&P 500 will reach 8,700 points in the next 12 months. On Nov. 20, the index closed at about 6,910, so this would imply a gain of about 26% from that level.

That's not an outlandish return to expect for the S&P 500. A 26% gain in 12 months would be an exceptionally strong year, much better than the average. But it's well within the realm of possibility.

Keep in mind that the long-term average annual return for the S&P 500, based on historical data since 1928, is 9.98%. And in the past few years, the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -3.42%) has delivered annual returns of about 17.8% in 2025, 25% in 2024, and 26.3% in 2023.

Why does Goldman think the S&P 500 will do so well in the next 12 months? If so many investors are nervous about tech companies spending too much on AI data centers, isn't now a bad time to buy the S&P? After all, as of Nov. 19, eight of the top 10 holdings in the S&P 500 were tech names, and those top eight stocks made up about 33.5% of the index by weight.

Recent investor skepticism about AI stocks might actually be healthy for future returns. Recent declines in tech stock prices have driven down those companies' valuations. Goldman's research finds that AI hyperscalers are trading "well below" their historic valuations and says that "we see Technology -- and Hyperscalers in particular -- as relatively defensive."

The very same tech stocks that have been going through a correction in the past month might be poised for bigger gains in the next 12 months. Goldman says that even though some of the biggest hyperscalers have negative cash flow due to their aggressive AI investments, these companies still have strong balance sheets and could become attractive in the next 12 months if growth opportunities elsewhere are harder to find.

This trend of lower valuations can also be seen if you look at the S&P 500 index forward price-to-earnings ratio (P/E). This metric shows the index's earnings multiple based on the next 12 months' estimated earnings. The S&P forward P/E has dropped from a recent high of 30 to 21.08, trading at about a 15% discount compared to its five-year average.

S&P 500 P/E Ratio Forward Estimate data by YCharts

Long-term investors need to think beyond today, tomorrow, or even the next 12 months. One year is not "long term." But this Goldman Sachs research is a helpful sign that there are many reasons to be optimistic about the stock market, despite some recent volatility.

If you share Goldman's optimism about the S&P 500 in 2027, now can be a good time to buy low-cost index funds like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO). This ultra-low-cost ETF lets you track the performance of the S&P.

You don't have to pick stocks or worry about which companies or industries are winning or losing; this Vanguard ETF lets you own 500 of the largest publicly traded companies in America, representing about 80% of the total U.S. stock market. If Goldman's analysis is on target, your investment in the ETF could go up by 26% in the next 12 months.

But even if that forecast doesn’t come true, you can rest assured that the S&P 500 has delivered strong average annual returns of about 10% for long-term investors who can buy and hold through temporary ups and downs along the way.

#3093. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#3089. Chipotle Mexican Grill vs. Hershey: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Hershey trades at a lower forward P/E than Chipotle, but the gap raises a bigger question about which stock is the better buy.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 8:45PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Chipotle Mexican Grill ›

Choosing between Chipotle Mexican Grill (CMG -2.07%) and Hershey (HSY +0.82%) depends on whether you value aggressive expansion or reliable brand power.

Chipotle leads the fast-casual space with its focus on fresh ingredients and digital efficiency. Hershey dominates the confectionery market with a massive portfolio of snacks and sweets. This comparison helps you decide which of these consumer giants better aligns with your financial goals heading into 2027.

Chipotle Mexican Grill focuses on its mission of serving real ingredients across more than 4,000 restaurants in the United States, Canada, Europe, and the Middle East. The company relies on a robust digital ordering platform that accounts for over 16% of food and beverage revenue, utilizing third-party delivery aggregators to reach more customers. It also expands its global presence through key partnerships, such as the one with Alshaya Group for international restaurant operations.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $12.7 billion, representing growth of approximately 7.9% compared to the prior period. The company reported net income of close to $1.4 billion, which reflects a net margin of approximately 10.8% for the period.

Hershey operates as a dominant force among consumer discretionary stocks through its massive portfolio of chocolate, sweets, and mints. The company sells its products through wholesale distributors and mass merchandisers, with McLane Company accounting for approximately 27% of consolidated net sales in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business since the loss of such a large partner could significantly impact overall sales.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 27, 2026, revenue reached close to $12.3 billion, an increase of roughly 6.7% over the previous 12 months. The company achieved net income of approximately $1.8 billion, which contributed to a net margin of nearly 14.6% during this timeframe.

Chipotle Mexican Grill faces significant risks related to food safety and potential food-borne illness incidents, which can severely damage its brand reputation and increase operating costs. The company also deals with intense competition from traditional restaurants and newer entities like ghost kitchens or delivery services such as Starbucks (SBUX -1.28%). Furthermore, the business must navigate legal and regulatory challenges regarding labor practices.

Hershey is sensitive to the availability and pricing volatility of key raw materials such as cocoa, sugar, and dairy. Supply chain disruptions caused by climate change or natural disasters could negatively impact its manufacturing operations across its global facilities. The company also faces pressure to successfully implement digital transformation and strategic business realignment programs to stay competitive against other snack manufacturers in a rapidly evolving retail environment.

Hershey appears cheaper than Chipotle Mexican Grill based on traditional metrics.

Chipotle is a story about growth and Hershey is about rebounding profitability, and knowing that can help find what the best fit for a portfolio is. For Chipotle, revenue increased 9.6% in the third quarter of 2026 to $3.3 billion. Transactions increased by 0.8%, comparable sales rose 2.1%, and digital sales jumped 38.8%. On top of that, Chipotle opened 84 new locations and expects to open up to 370 new restaurants by the end of 2026. That said, its operating margin dropped from 15.9% to 13.7%, and earnings per share dipped 6.9% despite the noticeable increase in revenue growth. Overall, while revenue growth and location expansion look impressive, shareholders will want to make sure higher costs aren't cutting too deeply into profitability.

For Hershey in its third quarter, while revenue increased only 3%, adjusted earnings per share (EPS) rose 103% and net income climbed 112%. Gross margin also increased from 32.6% to 46.1%. That said, organic sales increased only 0.8%, while international sales fell 7.5%. What Hershey is seeing appears to be improved results driven by better margins and lower costs, rather than pure consumer demand.

Between the two, if you prioritize more long-term sales growth potential, Chipotle may be the better fit for a portfolio. For anyone looking for a more mature business model with improving profitability, Hershey may be the better option.

#3088. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#3087. Amazon's re:Invent Conference Starts Nov. 30. Here's What the Stock Did in the Month After the Last 10.

The cloud giant's biggest week of the year is nearly here. Has the stock usually climbed after it?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 20, 2026 at 8:52PM EST

Amazon (AMZN -4.10%) has a big week ahead. Its cloud computing arm, Amazon Web Services (AWS), hosts its yearly re:Invent conference in Las Vegas from Monday, Nov. 30, to Friday, Dec. 4. AWS calls re:Invent its flagship annual conference, and it's where the unit launches many new products every year.

It's tempting to think the stock might get a boost when AWS shows off what it's built. To find out, I checked how Amazon shares did in the month after each of the past 10 conferences, from 2016 through 2025, and compared that with the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%).

Shares trade near $257 as I write, up about 12% this year and well ahead of the S&P 500's rise of about 5%. Amazon now stands about 9% below its Aug. 3 record close of $284.02.

Not really. For every year, I measured from the close on the trading day before the conference started to the close a month after opening day, using the same window for the S&P 500.

Amazon stock rose in just four of the 10 windows. It beat the index in four of them, too. And its median return was a drop of around 0.7%, but the S&P 500's median was a gain of about 2.1%.

The best year was 2024, when Amazon climbed around 6% in the month after the conference while the index dropped about 3%.

The worst was 2022. Other factors can easily outweigh a week of product news, and that year the stock had already lost about 44% before the conference began. It slid another 12% in the month that followed, compared with a 6% decline in the S&P 500.

Last year was closer to a wash. After the event ran from Dec. 1 to Dec. 5, 2025, Amazon stock slipped about 1% for the month, while the index was about flat.

In short, there's no consistent post-conference rally in the record. Amazon has lagged the S&P 500 more often than not after re:Invent -- in six of those 10 windows.

The week of the conference hasn't been much better. Measured from the previous close to that Friday's close, Amazon stock fell during six of those 10 conference weeks.

The 2024 event makes the strongest case that AWS's news can matter. That year, AWS rolled out its Nova family of artificial intelligence (AI) models and made servers built with its Trainium2 AI chips available to customers. Amazon shares climbed every trading day that week and gained around 9%, while the S&P 500 added about 1%.

But last year's lineup was arguably just as big. AWS made servers built with its next-generation Trainium3 chips available and unveiled its Nova 2 models, and the stock dipped about 2% in the conference week.

What does seem to move Amazon stock is its quarterly reports. Shares surged around 15% on July 31, the day after Amazon released its second-quarter results. In one day, the stock moved more than it did in any of those 10 post-conference months.

That makes sense, because AWS is where most of Amazon's profit comes from.

In the third quarter of 2026, AWS posted $17.8 billion in operating income, up 55% year over year. This was around 63% of Amazon's $28.1 billion in total operating income for the period.

Plus AWS's sales growth keeps accelerating. Its revenue rose 39% in the third quarter, faster than the second quarter's 37% and almost twice the 20% growth AWS had a year earlier.

Of course, all this takes heavy spending. Amazon still expects around $220 billion in cash capital expenditures in 2026, and that could weigh on its free cash flow for a while. And the stock isn't cheap, at about 25 times earnings based on what analysts expect for 2027. But with AWS growing almost twice as fast as it was a year ago, I think the price is fair.

So, is the month after re:Invent a good time to own Amazon stock? Going by the last 10 years, not especially. After all, four weeks is a short window, and I wouldn't use the record as a reason to rush in before the conference or to wait till after it.

I'd consider buying Amazon shares for the growth AWS keeps posting every quarter. I just wouldn't time my purchase around Nov. 30.

#3085. Crowd Pleaser….Crowdstrike Wants to Buy Zscaler

Here’s why this could be a game changer for cybersecurity investors.

By Randy Coon – Updated Nov 20, 2026 at 9:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than CrowdStrike ›

Two cybersecurity giants could be joining forces. But would a deal make CrowdStrike a better investment?

The cybersecurity industry may be on the verge of a blockbuster deal.

According to reports, CrowdStrike (CRWD -5.76%) is in talks to acquire Zscaler (ZS -5.29%), potentially bringing together two of the industry's most innovative companies in a transaction that could reshape the competitive landscape.

Investors reacted quickly sending Zscaler shares soaring roughly 17% in after-hours trading, while CrowdStrike shares fell about 8%.

For long-term investors, the question is what this combination could mean five or ten years from now.

CrowdStrike built its reputation protecting endpoints; the laptops, servers, and devices businesses rely on every day. Its Falcon platform uses artificial intelligence to identify and stop threats.

Zscaler approaches cybersecurity from another angle, pioneering zero-trust security that helps businesses safely connect employees to applications and data.

Put them together, and you potentially have something special: a comprehensive security platform protecting devices, users, networks, and cloud applications.

This is an incredibly powerful combo because cybersecurity customers increasingly want fewer vendors, simpler solutions, and better protection. And this combo creates an enormous opportunity.

First, cross-selling. CrowdStrike could introduce Zscaler's capabilities to its existing customers, while Zscaler could do the same with Falcon.

Second, competitive advantage. A combined company could become a more formidable competitor to Palo Alto Networks (PANW -5.09%) , strengthening its position in an increasingly consolidated industry.

Third, artificial intelligence. As businesses deploy more AI-powered applications and autonomous agents, protecting their digital infrastructure becomes increasingly complicated and increasingly essential.

Great acquisitions aren't automatically great investments. Zscaler carried a market capitalization of roughly $32 billion before the reports surfaced. That's a substantial price tag, and integrating two large technology platforms isn't easy.

CrowdStrike would also need to demonstrate that the benefits justify the acquisition premium, financing costs, and potential shareholder dilution.

Cybersecurity isn't a passing trend. It's becoming essential infrastructure for the digital economy. A CrowdStrike-Zscaler combination could create a powerhouse positioned to benefit from that transformation for years. But remember: A great business isn't always a great stock at any price.

Before getting excited, Foolish investors should watch the purchase price, financing structure, and management's plans for generating long-term value. Because ultimately, it's not the size of the deal that matters. It's the returns it delivers to shareholders. Both Zscaler and CrowdStrike are official Motley Fool recommendations. #3084. The S&P 500 Just Entered Correction Territory. Here's What It Means for Investors Right Now.

After plunging 8.6% for the week, the S&P 500 is now in a correction.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) just wrapped up one of its worst weeks in history, falling 8.6% as the AI trade collapsed. The index gave up 3.7% on Friday to close in correction territory, defined as a decline of 10% or more from a recent closing high. It was the first time the index had fallen below 7,000 since April.

Corrections are fairly routine events on the stock market. On average, the S&P 500 enters a correction about every 1.5 years, and its last correction came in March-April of 2025 as concerns about President Trump's tariffs and a trade war drove investors to sell.

Nervousness around the AI sector has been pushing tech stocks lower since the beginning of October, but the negative sentiment didn't really hit the S&P 500 until this week.

Investors are worried about rising interest rates squeezing the AI infrastructure build-out, including in the unprofitable neocloud sector; the backlash against data centers following the midterm elections; and the crisis unfolding at Nvidia after Jensen Huang resigned, with investigations into chip exports to China and now an accounting investigation by the SEC.

Fears of an AI bubble have been swirling in the market for a while, but despite some of the air coming out of these stocks, the fundamentals still look strong and valuations remain cheap.

Of the last 10 corrections in the S&P 500, which go back to 2011, the average decline has been 17%, and two of those turned into bear markets. That happened during the COVID crash early in 2020 and in 2022, a bear market that lasted most of the year.

The average duration from peak to a new peak was 9.4 months, and the average duration from the bottom to a new peak was 6.1 months, though the last two corrections took less than 100 days.

Regardless of the length of time, the S&P 500 has recovered from every correction it's experienced.

A correction is no reason to panic, and it's a regular part of stock market volatility. However, this is a good time to put together a watch list of stocks you'd like to buy if you have cash available. If you are a net buyer of stocks, a sell-off is actually a good thing, as Warren Buffett advised, as it makes stocks cheaper to buy.

Some quality stocks like Alphabet and Meta now have price-to-earnings ratios below 20, making them look attractive, and we're likely to see more deals if stocks fall further.

If you're not in a position to buy stocks, it's worth remembering that historically, the S&P 500 returns to an all-time high within nine months on average. How this sell-off plays out will depend on the AI sector, but the low valuations for many of the top AI stocks are an encouraging sign.

#3081. If the Nasdaq's Correction Turns Into a Bear Market, This Is the First Stock I'll Buy

Another 7.5% fall would send the index into a bear market. Which battered giant would I buy first?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 20, 2026 at 10:57PM EST

The Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -5.28%) finished at 23,879 on Thursday, Nov. 19. The tech-heavy index now sits about 13.5% below its Oct. 6 record close of 27,600 -- a correction, though not a bear market.

The standard line for a bear market is a 20% fall from the high. For the Nasdaq, that's a close near 22,080, or about another 7.5% drop from Thursday's level.

I'm not predicting the index gets there. But a plan is easier to stick to when it's written down before a sell-off than during one, and mine is this: If the Nasdaq closes in bear-market territory, the first stock I'll buy is Alphabet (GOOGL -2.70%)(GOOG -2.59%).

Shares of the Google parent closed at $272.66 on Thursday, their lowest close since October 2025. The stock's now about 32% below where it finished the day on May 13, at a record close of $402.62 -- more than double the Nasdaq's decline from its own high.

A lot of that drop came in a single session. The stock fell about 13% on Oct. 29, the day after Alphabet posted third-quarter results showing slower Search growth, lower earnings per share, and a second straight quarter of negative free cash flow.

In other words, the stock's arguably already taken the sort of hit a bear market brings, while the business underneath keeps growing.

Showing how well the business has held up through the drop, Alphabet's revenue growth has accelerated all year. Sales grew 22% year over year in the first quarter, then 24% in the second, and third-quarter sales rose 25% to $127.6 billion. Operating income grew 30% year over year in all three quarters. And in the first nine months of 2026, operating income came to $121 billion, a 30% jump from $93 billion a year before.

I focus on operating income because Alphabet's net income swings with the value of its stakes in other companies. Other income (mainly investment gains) added around $127 billion in the same nine months.

That said, not every number is heading the right way. Last year's third quarter had a $3.5 billion European Commission fine, so without it, operating income rose more like 17% in the latest quarter. And as spending on artificial intelligence (AI) shows up in costs, the operating margin has shrunk. It was 31.8% in the third quarter, versus 34% in the April-to-June period and 36.1% in the first quarter.

The second reason is the balance sheet. Alphabet expects to spend between $195 billion and $205 billion on capital expenditures in 2026, and management said that amount is expected to rise significantly in 2027. A bear market could make raising money for that sort of spending more expensive.

But Alphabet has already raised a lot of it. In the first nine months of 2026, the company raised about $85 billion in new debt, about $43 billion by selling common stock, and another $19 billion by issuing preferred stock.

Alphabet's cash and marketable securities totaled $265.1 billion at the end of September, more than twice its total at the end of 2025. Long-term debt was $125.1 billion.

Yes, selling new shares dilutes current shareholders, and free cash flow was negative in both of the past two quarters. But with around $140 billion more in cash and marketable securities than in long-term debt, Alphabet arguably doesn't need a steady stock market to keep funding its build-out.

Alphabet stock doesn't look expensive to me now. At $272.66, it costs around 18 times expected 2027 earnings, while Meta Platforms, another ad-driven tech giant, trades at about 17 times its expected 2027 earnings. For a business growing operating income by double digits, I think the price is fair.

I wouldn't argue with anyone buying some now. The reason to wait for the line is that a bear market would probably mark down many stocks at once. Deciding beforehand which one gets the first dollar stops me from chasing whatever happened to fall the most that week.

Alphabet doesn't have to track the index, either. But if it just matched the Nasdaq's remaining 7.5% drop from Thursday's close, the stock would trade near $252, or about 17 times expected 2027 earnings.

If the Nasdaq closes 20% below its record, Alphabet is likely where my plan begins.

Sure, the index might never get there. And if it doesn't, Alphabet at around 18 times expected 2027 earnings still looks fairly priced to me.

#3079. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#3076. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#3075. Follow the Smart Money: 4 AI Stocks Billionaire Dan Loeb Just Bought

Hedge fund manager Dan Loeb was buying shares of Nvidia, Space Exploration Technologies, Microsoft, and Meta Platforms in Q3 2026.

By Geoffrey Seiler – Nov 21, 2026 at 12:00AM EST

Dan Loeb is widely considered one of the world's top investors, starting his Third Point hedge fund with a few million dollars in 1995 and turning it into an over-$28 billion behemoth. In Q3 2026, he was busy scooping up several artificial intelligence (AI) stocks, including Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%), Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -7.69%), Microsoft (MSFT -2.77%), and Meta Platforms (META -5.93%).

Let's look at what may have attracted Loeb to these names.

Loeb's buy of Nvidia shares came before CEO Jensen Huang stepped down, but the company remains in the pole position in the AI infrastructure build-out. Nvidia today is more than just a graphics processing unit (GPU) provider, offering a complete AI factory solution that includes networking, central processing units (CPUs), language processing units (LPUs) for the decode phase of inference, its Nemotron open-weight models, and an expanding software layer.

That package has helped Nvidia increase its revenue per gigawatt from around $18 billion when it introduced its Hopper architecture a few years ago to around $40 billion today with Vera Rubin.

With future memory and manufacturing capacity locked in, along with its strong ecosystem, expect Nvidia to remain the AI infrastructure leader over the coming years.

SpaceX is one of the most exciting companies on the planet as it looks to disrupt industries and conquer space. The company recently achieved a major milestone by catching the upper stage of its Starship rocket, setting it up to fundamentally change the economics of space launches as it moves toward rapid reuse. This is also a stepping stone for SpaceX to hit some of its loftier goals, such as orbital data centers, Earth-to-Earth transport, and asteroid mining.

Back on Earth, SpaceX has become one of the world's largest neocloud companies and plans to rapidly grow its AI infrastructure-as-a-service business. Right now, it is getting great rates on its AI infrastructure through short-term rentals, allowing it to see a payback on its investments within a year. The company also plans to disrupt the U.S. mobile market, and its Cursor AI coding platform is growing quickly.

Microsoft continues to see strong growth in both its Azure cloud computing unit and its enterprise software business. Azure has been a persistent 30%-plus revenue grower, with its revenue growth hitting 48% last quarter. With significant commitments from OpenAI and an overall $677 billion backlog, Azure should remain a strong growth driver in the years ahead.

At the same time, the company has been gaining significant traction with its AI Copilot assistants across Microsoft 365 and its GitHub code repository. The shift to a seat-plus-consumption model has been paying off, and Copilot usage has been surging, with it seeing an eightfold year-over-year increase in seats last quarter. Given how embedded the company's software and governance tools are in the enterprise space, it looks well positioned to emerge as an AI software-as-a-service (SaaS) winner.

Meta Platforms has been one of the best companies at using AI to drive growth in an already established, strong business. It's employing AI to both keep users on its apps longer by displaying more content they are interested in and to feed them more relevant ads. This is leading to higher ad loads, stronger conversions, and better ad pricing.

Last quarter, its revenue surged 26%, with a 14% increase in average price per ad and a 10% jump in ad impressions.

Perhaps more exciting, though, is that Meta is jumping headfirst into consumer AI agents. These agents can help users with a multitude of tasks, but Meta will benefit most when they make purchases on a user's behalf. Here, Meta will get a cut of the transaction fee, giving it another potential new large revenue stream and putting it at the forefront of agentic commerce.

#3072. Pick Six: New York Jets at Los Angeles Chargers

The New York Jets are, embarrassingly, a three-point underdog on Sunday against a team who didn't earn their first win until less than a week ago. The Los Angeles Chargers will be looking to build on their 23-20 win over the Ravens, and the Jets are making the long trip out west to take them on.

Here are six storylines to watch as New York seeks to inject some positivity into their season:

Last week, we discussed the Jets' varying options for their slot cornerback position and they eventually went with one of our suggestions, which was to primarily use Minkah Fitzpatrick there in nickel packages, bringing Malachi Moore off the bench to fill in at safety.

The Jets potentially face a similar dilemma in the slot this week, albeit on offense. Both Omar Cooper and Isaiah Williams have been unable to practice, so if neither can go on Sunday, the Jets will have to make a change.

One option could be to go with the guys currently on the roster. You could reactivate Jelani Woods and Tim Patrick and either run more multiple tight end packages, or use players like Garrett Wilson there. Another option could be to bring up Sterling Shepard or Jamaal Pritchett from the practice squad.

The job share at running back remains a work in progress

The Jets are still trying to figure out how to get the best out of Braelon Allen and Breece Hall now that both are healthy at the same time. Allen's performances while Hall was out proved that he can benefit from a bigger workload. However, Hall brings different things to the table in the passing game, so the Jets will want him on the field more often than not.

Last week was interesting because they employed Hall more in the passing game in the first half with Allen getting more carries. At the half, Allen had six carries and Hall had just two. However, after halftime, Allen carried the ball just twice and Hall had nine carries. It's possible the Jets' thinking was that Hall might produce better after halftime if he was fresh and the defense was tired. In the end, the pair had similar rushing numbers, but Hall produced more as a receiver.

You can probably expect the Jets to keep working on this with one option being flipping last week's gameplan on its head and using Hall early, then keeping Allen fresh to be the hammer in the second half. The Chargers' run defense did a great job against the Ravens, holding them to 16 yards on 15 carries, so it might not be easy to get a running game established.

The Chargers' leading tackler is linebacker Daiyan Henley, but he's been managing a knee issue all season and has been missing practice this week.

Henley, who is also second on the team in defensive snaps played, has been exploited in coverage at times this year, but is still a key contributor to the Chargers' run defense and tends to be the guy who wears the defensive headset.

If Henley can't go, the next man up would be Marlowe Wax, who has played under 100 defensive snaps in his career.

Chargers quarterback Justin Herbert has been struggling in the passing game. A large part of that is that the Chargers have struggled to protect him. Herbert has been sacked 33 times so far this season; more than every NFL quarterback apart from Deshaun Watson. Only two of their starting offensive linemen have started all nine games, which hasn't helped matters.

Last week, the Jets increased Will McDonald's workload as they had promised to, but it meant that Kingsley Enagbare only got to rush the passer a season-low 15 times. With Joseph Ossai now also back in the rotation, sustaining McDonald's level of opportunities might prove tricky. He did respond with a sack last week, though.

The Jets's run defense continues to struggle with two of their starting interior defensive linemen on injured reserve, although one – David Onyemata – was activated for practice this week, so is eligible to return.

New York gave up over 200 yards to the Bills, including a back-breaking 74-yard touchdown in the fourth quarter. The damage could have been even worse had it not been for the efforts of Harrison Phillips, though, as he stopped 10 runs for a gain of four or less.

Los Angeles has the NFL's worst scoring offense but they actually boast a top-10 running game and are coming off a game against the Ravens where Omarion Hampton rushed for over 100 yards for the first time all year. In addition, Herbert gave them a boost with a season-high 51 rushing yards, including his first touchdown.

When the Jets won games last year, a feature of these performances was good production in the return game but that hasn't been happening in 2026. If Williams is out this week, maybe that wouldn't be the worst thing, because he's been much less productive than last season.

In last week's game, the Jets used Isaiah Davis on kickoffs and he did quite well, having always put up solid numbers in the past. If Williams is out, then they may elevate someone like Pritchett to return punts, although the depth chart on the team's official website lists Davis as the backup punt returner too.

The bad news is that the Chargers' coverage numbers are pretty solid, led by ex-Jets linebacker Del'Shawn Phillips who was the NFL's leader in special teams tackles last season. On kickoffs, Cameron Dicker has allowed the third lowest return average in the NFL and their punter JK Scott is the number one graded punter according to Pro Football Focus rankings.

#3071. How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch

How LeBron James dissected Pacers defense down the stretch originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

As he overtook Jason Kidd for the third-most assists in NBA history, LeBron James piloted the Sixers' offense down the stretch against the Indiana Pacers on Sunday.

With Joel Embiid sitting out and Tyrese Maxey getting a breather on the bench, James took over in the fourth quarter. He did so by spamming the same action over and over again.

The set consisted of James getting a ball screen from a guard – either VJ Edgecombe or Anfernee Simons – at the top of the key. After setting or ghosting the screen, the guard would short roll towards the basket.

When the Pacers blitzed or hedged the screen, James found the guard. The Sixers could then attack a 4-on-3 or 3-on-2 situation.

Twice, Edgecombe and Simons scored off the roll.

When the Pacers helped to the roller, that opened up the shooters in each corner.

James directed traffic on offense to ensure that the Sixers spaced the floor properly. Here, he waved Dean Wade out of the dunker spot and into the corner. Edgecombe promptly hit him for an open three off the short roll.

After all of the Sixers' success, the Pacers eventually switched the ball screen. That left the 6-foot-4, 173-pound Quenton Jackson on James, who has five inches and 77 pounds on him.

Jackson and James had already gone head to head on Sunday.

In the first quarter, James set a ball screen for Edgecombe which the Pacers switched. Edgecombe fed James in the post and the Pacers doubled him to support Jackson. James punished them for it.

They didn't double James after the switch in the fourth quarter. This time, James backed Jackson down, backed him down again and backed him down some more before scoring inside.

James' strength abusing matchups against smaller defenders after switches has been a safety valve for the Sixers' offense. Guards struggle to stop him from getting to the basket. When defenses send help, he picks them apart with his otherworldly passing ability.

That combination forced the Pacers to pick their poison. From the 9:55 mark in the fourth until the Pacers switched to a zone defense (after James bullied Jackson) with just over 6:00 left, the Sixers scored 13 points out of this action across seven possessions. That's a stellar 1.86 points per possession.

James, who's scoring a career-low 15.1 points per game so far this season, might not be the elite bucket-getter he was for most of his career. But he's still brilliant at conducting highly efficient offense that pressures a defense's pain points.
